Different Gaussian wave packets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|\Phi _{i}\rangle =\psi _{E_c,x_c,\sigma _x}(x,t_I)$$\end{document} used in the numerical simulations in this work and defined in Eq. (N50), in Online Appendix N, in terms of the central energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_c$$\end{document}, central wave vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_c=\sqrt{2m^*E/\hbar ^2}$$\end{document}, central velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_c=\frac{\hbar k_c}{m^*},$$\end{document} central position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c$$\end{document}, and spatial dispersion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _x$$\end{document} at the wave packet preparation time.

When C4 is satisfied

In Fig. 2a, the pre-selected state is a Gaussian wave packet with a normal (not too short and not too large) spatial dispersion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle =|\Phi _{1}\rangle$$\end{document}, while the post-selected state is the Gaussian wave packet with a very short spatial dispersion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Phi _{2}|$$\end{document}, as seen in Table 2. The last Gaussian wave packet is so narrow that it mimics a position eigenstate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|=\langle \Phi _{2}|\approx \langle x_c|$$\end{document}. The theoretical LHD in Eq. (13) for the position operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document}, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_R=0$$\end{document}, can be written as:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \left. \frac{ \partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}},t_L,0\big ||x_c \rangle ,|\Phi _{1}\rangle \big )}{\partial t_L}\right| _{ t_L =0}=\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,0\big ||x_c\rangle ,|\Phi _{1}\rangle \big )\nonumber \\ & \qquad +Re \left\{ {\frac{i}{\hbar }\frac{\langle x_c |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}\hat{{H}}| \Phi _{1}\rangle }{\langle x_c |\Phi _{1}\rangle }-\frac{i}{\hbar }\frac{\langle x_c | \hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\Phi _{1} \rangle }{\langle x_c | \Phi _{1}\rangle }\frac{\langle x_c |\hat{{H}}|\Phi _{1}\rangle }{\langle x_c |\Phi _{1}\rangle }} \right \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The numerical evaluation of inner products in Eq. The results are plotted in a solid red line in Fig. 2a at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_R=t_L=0$$\end{document}. Since C4 is satisfied, LHD is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,0\big ||v_o\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )=Re \left\{ {\frac{\langle x_c |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\Phi _{1}\rangle }{\langle x_c |\Phi _{1}\rangle }} \right\}={\textbf {v}}_B^{|\Phi _{1}\rangle }(x_c,0)\approx {\hbar k_{c,1}}/{m^*}=2.28\cdot 10^5$$\end{document} m/s which corresponds to the Bohmian velocity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x_c|\Phi _{1}(0)\rangle$$\end{document}. The formal development leading to the definition of the Bohmian velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_B^{|\Phi _{1}\rangle }$$\end{document} will be elaborated in detail later in the development of Eq. (25) (see also Eq. (27)).

The final results in Eq. (21) are plotted in red in Fig. 2a for different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L$$\end{document}. The numerical values of results LHD and FDLHD are roughly identical, as justified in Online Appendix F when C4 is satisfied.

The values of RHD and FDRHD are also computed numerically with similar procedures, giving both values close to zero as plotted in blue in Fig. 2a. Such results can be easily justified through condition Eq. (17) rewritten here as LHD+RHD\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=Re \left\{ { \frac{\langle x_c | \frac{i}{\hbar }[\hat{{H}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}] |\Phi _{1}\rangle }{\langle x_c |\Phi _{1}\rangle }} \right\}={\textbf {v}}_B^{|\Phi _{1}\rangle }(x_c,0)$$\end{document}, which fixes RHD to zero because we have already shown that LHD gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_B^{|\Phi _{1}\rangle }(x_c,0)$$\end{document}. The value FDRHD is also zero because it satisfies a similar relationship with FDLHD as shown in Eq. (G36) in Online Appendix G.

Notice that the results in Fig. 2a have been computed for different angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}. These angles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document} parametrize different gauge functions g(x, t) as described in Eq. (N56) in Online Appendix N. The initial pre- and post-selected states are the ones mentioned above multiplied by the gauge function at their respective initial time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{i\frac{q}{\hbar }g(x,0)}\langle x|\Phi _{1}(0)\rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-i\frac{q}{\hbar }g(x,t_L)} \langle \Phi _{2}(t_L) |x\rangle$$\end{document}, as described in Eq. (N57) in Online Appendix N and their time evolution is given now by the algorithm described in Eq. (N63) in Online Appendix N. Here, all the final outputs become gauge independent (i.e, they do not depend on the selected gauge function parametrized by the angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}). The FDLHD and FDRHD are gauge independent by construction, while LHD and RHD are gauge independent here because condition C4 is satisfied.

When C5 is satisfied

In Fig. 2b, we plot similar results for a scenario where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|$$\end{document} are interchanged. The theoretical RHD in Eq. (15), when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} (\hat{{\textbf {X}}},0,t_R\big ||\Phi _{1} \rangle ,|x_c\rangle \big )}{\partial t_R}\right| _{ t_R =0}=\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,0\big || \Phi _{1}\rangle ,|x_c\rangle \big )\nonumber In Fig. 2b, in blue, we show that the results RHD and FDRHD are roughly identical. The condition Eq. (17) and Eq. (G36) also fix the values of LHD and FDLHD to roughly zero. The reasons for the gauge invariance (independence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}) are equivalent to the ones explained for Fig. 2a. Now, the accomplishment of condition C5 is involved.

When C4 and C5 are not satisfied

The next analysis provided in Fig. 2c explores a different scenario where neither the pre-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Phi _{3}\rangle$$\end{document} nor the post-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Phi _{4}|$$\end{document} can be considered position eigenstates. Thus, since neither condition C4 nor C5 is satisfied, the LHD computed from Eq. (20) and RHD computed from Eq. (22) are gauge dependent as seen for the red and blue curves at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L=t_R=0$$\end{document}, i.e., their values depend on the angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document} which parametrizes different gauge functions g(x, t) as described in Eq. (N56) in Online Appendix N. On the contrary, by construction, the FDLHD computed from Eq. (21) and FDRHD computed from Eq. (23) remain gauge invariant as described in Online Appendies F and G but their values coincide neither with the Bohmian velocity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Phi _{3}\rangle$$\end{document} nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \Phi _{4}|$$\end{document} written in Table 2.

In green, we have plotted LHD+RHD at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_R=t_L=0$$\end{document} and FDLHD+FDRHD for different values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_R$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}. Both sums become equal and gauge invariant as indicated in Eq. (17) and Eq. (G36) (for the time-independent operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document}), but the value of the sum does not coincide with the sum of the Bohmian velocities. In summary, when neither condition C4 nor C5 are satisfied, LHD and RHD cannot be obtained in the laboratory. Finally, it is interesting to revisit Eq. (21) to acquire an intuitive motivation for C4. It seems natural to define the time derivative of a weak value as the difference between two weak values: one weak value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c$$\end{document} evaluated without time evolution, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{U}_{{t_L}}=1\!\!1$$\end{document}, and the other weak value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_L$$\end{document} with a time evolution during the time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L$$\end{document}. It seems obvious to define the post-selected position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c$$\end{document}, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{U}_{{t_L}}=1\!\!1$$\end{document} in terms of weak values as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c={\langle x_c |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}| \psi \rangle }/{\langle x_c |\psi \rangle }$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|x_c\rangle =x_c|x_c\rangle$$\end{document}. Thus, the weak value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_L$$\end{document} with arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{U}_{{t_L}}$$\end{document} has to be defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_L={\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}| \psi \rangle }/{\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document}, and we conclude that C4 becomes a quite natural requirement for the time derivative of a weak value because the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|x_c\rangle =x_c|x_c\rangle$$\end{document}, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\hat{{O}},\hat{{F}}]=0$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{F}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document}, is required at the origin of this argumentation. This is exactly what happens for the LHD in Eq. (13) when C4 is satisfied. An identical conclusion occurs for C5 from Eq. (23). All these results regarding the gauge condition and the shape of the theoretical, LHD and RHD, and empirical, FDLHD and FDRHD, weak values are summarized in Table 3.

Examples on Gauge invariance of weak values

After the theoretical development and finite-difference approximations done in Sections “Time Derivative of Weak values” and “Finite-difference approximations”, here, we show several weak values often mentioned in the literature. In particular, we discuss whether or not they satisfy the gauge invariance implicit in properties C1, C3. To simplify the discussion, we will assume that the condition C2 in the preparation of the initial state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi ^g\rangle$$\end{document} is always satisfied. Since this section does not deal with time-derivative yet, we will use Eq. (12) for the evaluation of the weak values, rather than Eq. (11).

To properly understand the results in this section, first, we need to clarify what we mean when we say, for example, that the canonical momentum on Table 1 is not empirically observable in the laboratory because it is gauge dependent. It is well-known that the canonical momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {P}}}^g:= \hat{{\textbf {P}}}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g},\hat{A^g}) = \hat{{\textbf {P}}}\ne \hat{{G}}\hat{{\textbf {P}}}\hat{{G}}^{\dagger }$$\end{document} does not satisfy condition C1. Thus, strictly speaking, it cannot be empirically measured in the laboratory either from an expectation value or from a weak value in a direct way. However, in many experimental works, the measurement of the “momentum” is inferred from the measurement of another property that is gauge invariant. For example, by measuring the position of the quantum system on the screen73 and knowing the initial and final time of the experiment. As we will discuss below, this measuring procedure is a measurement of the velocity (or the velocity multiplied by the mass) rather than a direct measurement of the canonical momentum (we emphasize that the above discussions and all the results in this paper about non-measurable gauge-dependent results do not imply ontological consequences for the canonical momentum or other gauge-dependent properties.).

In other words, the fact that the electromagnetic potentials are not gauge invariant does not imply that they are not physically relevant 51, but only that they cannot be directly measured in the laboratory.

Weak value of the canonical momentum post-selected in position

As discussed above, the canonical momentum operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {P}}}^g:=\hat{{\textbf {P}}}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g},\hat{A^g})=\hat{{\textbf {P}}}\ne \hat{{G}}\hat{{\textbf {P}}}\hat{{G}}^{\dagger }$$\end{document} does not satisfy condition C1 and cannot be measured through weak values. Let us confirm this point by writing the theoretical expression corresponding to the weak measurement of the canonical momentum plus a subsequent strong measurement of the position operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}^g:=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g},\hat{A^g})=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}=\hat{{G}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}\hat{{G}}^{\dagger }$$\end{document}. Such weak value would be given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {P}}}\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} which, when written in any gauge, becomes24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {\textbf {x}}^g |\hat{{\textbf {P}}}|\psi ^g\rangle }{\langle {\textbf {x}}^g|\psi ^g\rangle }} \right \}=Re \left \{ {-i\hbar \frac{{\boldsymbol{\nabla }}\psi ^g}{\psi ^g}} \right \}={\boldsymbol{\nabla }}S-q {\boldsymbol{\nabla }}g. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\hat{{G}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}]=0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi ^g\rangle =\hat{{G}}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}. We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ({\textbf {x}},t)=R({\textbf {x}},t)e^{iS({\textbf {x}},t)/\hbar }$$\end{document} in polar form to better elaborate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\boldsymbol{\nabla }}\psi ^g}/{\psi ^g}$$\end{document}. The explicit dependence of Eq. (24) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\nabla }}g$$\end{document} confirms that this weak value cannot be observed in the laboratory.

In the Coulomb gauge representation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g=0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\nabla }}\cdot {\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}=0$$\end{document}), in the absence of a magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}=0$$\end{document}, the velocity and canonical momentum operators are identical \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\hat{{\textbf {V}}}=\hat{{\textbf {P}}}$$\end{document}. This coincidence in one particular gauge does not mean that the velocity and momentum operators are the same in general, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\hat{{\textbf {V}}}^g\ne \hat{{\textbf {P}}}^g$$\end{document}. In the same way, the knowledge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}=0$$\end{document} in the Coulomb gauge does not mean that one can assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g=0$$\end{document} in another gauge.

Weak value of the velocity post-selected in position

Contrary to the canonical momentum, the velocity operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}^g$$\end{document} is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{{\textbf {V}}}^g:=\hat{{\textbf {V}}}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g},\hat{A^g})=(\hat{{\textbf {P}}}^g-q\hat{{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}}^g)/m=\hat{{\textbf {V}}}=\hat{{G}}\hat{{\textbf {V}}}\hat{{G}}^{\dagger }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and it is gauge invariant and satisfies C1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}. Hence, the weak measurement of the velocity post-selected in position gives the empirically observable weak value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}}\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} defined as25The weak value gives the so-called Bohmian velocity5,6,9, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_B^{|\psi \rangle }=({\boldsymbol{\nabla }}S-q{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}})/m^*$$\end{document}, which is gauge invariant because it satisfies C1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document} and C3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{F}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document}. See an alternative demonstration in Online Appendix H and also74. The superscript \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document} in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_B^{|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document} just indicates that the Bohmian velocity is a functional of the wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi$$\end{document}.

As discussed previously (see71,72), another weak value can be designed to give the imaginary part of Eq. (25), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Im {{\langle {\textbf {x}}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}^g |\psi ^g\rangle }/{\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle }}$$\end{document}, which is called the “osmotic” velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_O^{|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document}9,58,75,76 and given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_O^{|\psi \rangle }:=-Im {{\langle {\textbf {x}}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}^g |\psi ^g\rangle }/{\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle }}=\frac{\hbar }{m^*}\frac{{\boldsymbol{\nabla }}R^g}{R^g}$$\end{document}, which is also gauge invariant because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^g=R$$\end{document}. Notice that there is some ambiguity regarding the sign in the definition of osmotic velocity in the literature9,58,75,76.

The consistency in interpreting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}}\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} as a velocity is provided by the LHD in Eq. (13) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textit{f}\;\rangle =|{\textbf {x}}\rangle$$\end{document}, as will be seen in Eq. (27) in next section.

Weak value of the position projector post-selected in the canonical momentum

Let us now analyze the weak value corresponding to the position projector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {x}}^g \rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}^g|=\hat{{G}}|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|\hat{{G}}^{\dagger }=|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|$$\end{document} post-selected by the canonical momentum operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {P}}}$$\end{document} defined as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}\big (|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|\big ||{\textbf {p}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )=\frac{{\langle {\textbf {p}}^g|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle }}{{\langle {\textbf {p}}^g|\psi ^g\rangle }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}It is argued in the literature36,37 that such a weak value, together with the corresponding conditional ensemble for the imaginary part71,72, is proportional to the wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle$$\end{document} when post-selected at zero momentum. Despite the fact that this weak value satisfies C1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|$$\end{document}, the selection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{F}}=\hat{{\textbf {P}}}^g$$\end{document}, to define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {p}}^g|$$\end{document}, does not satisfy C3 because the canonical momentum is gauge dependent. The gauge-dependent eigenstate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|{\textbf {p}}^g\rangle =e^{i(qg({\textbf {x}},t)+{\textbf {p}}{\textbf {x}})/\hbar }$$\end{document} has a gauge-dependent eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-i\hbar {\boldsymbol{\nabla }}\langle {\textbf {x}}|{\textbf {p}}^g\rangle =(g({\textbf {x}},t)+{\textbf {p}})\langle {\textbf {x}}|{\textbf {p}}^g\rangle$$\end{document}. Thus, this weak value of the canonical momentum cannot be obtained in the laboratory.

Weak value of the position projector post-selected in the velocity

It is true, however, that the non-measurability of the canonical momentum described above can be avoided by substituting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {P}}}$$\end{document} by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}}\big ||{\textbf {v}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document}, is given by26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\langle {\textbf {v}}^g|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle }{\langle {\textbf {v}}^g|\psi ^g\rangle } =\frac{R_{{\textbf {v}}} R_{\psi } e^{i\frac{S_{{\textbf {v}}}-S_{\psi }}{\hbar }}}{\langle {\textbf {v}}|\psi \rangle }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {v}}^g|{\textbf {x}}\rangle =R_{{\textbf {v}}} e^{i (S_{{\textbf {v}}}+qG)/\hbar }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle = R_{\psi } e^{i (S_{\psi }+qG)/\hbar }$$\end{document}. Clearly, this weak value satisfies C1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|$$\end{document} and C3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{F}}=\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}. Hence, the (phase of the) weak value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}}\big ||{\textbf {v}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} is now gauge invariant, as seen here: .

However, arguing that Eq. (26) measures the (phase of the) Hilbert space representation of the wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi ^g\rangle$$\end{document} is not rigorous, because all wave functions are gauge dependent and unmeasurable, as seen in Eq. (5). The argument in the literature is based on assuming that for a magnetic field equal to zero, in the Coulomb gauge, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\hat{{\textbf {V}}}=\hat{{\textbf {P}}}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {v}}|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \propto e^{i({\textbf {p}}{\textbf {x}})/\hbar }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi \rangle = R_{\psi } e^{i S_{\psi }/\hbar }$$\end{document}. Then, Eq. (26) gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (|{\textbf {x}}\rangle \langle {\textbf {x}}|\big ||{\textbf {v}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )_{{\textbf {v}}=0} \propto \langle {\textbf {x}}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}36. While this protocol, which effectively gives the wave function defined in the Coulomb gauge, can indeed be very useful for developing new quantum technologies, there is no reason to affirm that the Coulomb gauge representation of the wave function is the true wave function of the system, in the same way as the Coulomb-gauge representation of the electromagnetic potentials are not the true potentials. At the fundamental level, there should be no preferred gauge, and hence, the wave function and the potentials are unmeasurable, as indicated in Eqs. (3) and (5), respectively.

Examples on the time derivative of weak values

Here, we discuss three examples of the time derivative of weak values, dealing with local velocity, the local energy theorem, and the local Lorentz force. These numerical examples explicitly demonstrate the usefulness and abilities of time-dependent weak values.

We compute the time derivative of the weak value through numerical simulations. As discussed in Online Appendix N, the numerical simulations require spatial and temporal grids. Thus, strictly speaking, the time derivatives computed in this section correspond to the finite-difference expressions FDLHD and FDRHD outlined in Section “Finite-difference approximations”. In all simulations, we consider scenarios where conditions C4, C5, or both are satisfied so that the FDLHD and FDRHD can be interpreted as the LHD and RHD defined in Section “Time Derivative of Weak values”. In addition, in these numerical simulations, the time step is chosen to be sufficiently small so that the FDLHD and FDRHD provide excellent approximations to the LHD and RHD (the small numerical time step used in the numerical computations in this paper does not need to coincide with the experimental time step required to evaluate the time derivatives in the laboratory, which must be determined by the time interval over which relevant time-dependent variations occur in the quantum system.).

To gain additional physical insight into the behavior of such weak values and their time derivatives, after the numerical evaluation of the weak values, we re-interpret the results as Bohmian properties of a quantum system.

Local velocity of particle

Wiseman formulated the first attempt to discuss time derivatives of weak values5 when presenting a local velocity from the time-derivative of a weak value of the position (post-selected in position eigenstates).

In Eq. (25) we have found how the expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}}\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} can be interpreted as the local velocity of a quantum particle. The consistency in interpreting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}}\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )$$\end{document} as a velocity is provided by the LHD in Eq. (13) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textit{f}\;\rangle =|{\textbf {x}}\rangle$$\end{document}.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left. \frac{ \partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}},t_L,0\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big )}{\partial t_L}\right| _{t_L=0} =\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,0\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|\psi \rangle \big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}=\frac{i}{\hbar }[\hat{{H}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}]$$\end{document} as seen in Online Appendix I. Notice that Eq. (27) satisfies C4 (gauge invariance) because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\hat{{O}},\hat{{F}}]=[\hat{{\textbf {X}}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}]=0$$\end{document}. Thus, the local velocity can be obtained for an arbitrary pre-selected quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle$$\end{document} because the condition C4 just fixes the post-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|=\langle {\textbf {x}}|$$\end{document}. We only require a Hamiltonian that satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}=\frac{i}{\hbar }[\hat{{H}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}]$$\end{document}.

As suggested by Wiseman5, Eq. (27) shows that the Bohmian velocity is just the quantum version of the classical time-of-flight procedure to evalute the velocity: two positions are consecutively measured and the velocity defined as the distance divided by the time interval between the two measurements. In the discussion at the end of Section “Finite-difference approximations”, we have identified the first weak value as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c={\langle x_c |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}| \psi \rangle }/{\langle x_c |\psi \rangle }$$\end{document} and the second as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_L={\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}| \psi \rangle }/{\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document}. Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}| \psi \rangle }/{\langle x_c |\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }$$\end{document} can be understood as giving insight on which was the (weak value of the) position of the system at a time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L$$\end{document} earlier than the time when the (strong) position of the system is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c$$\end{document}. This reasoning implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_c=x_L+v_B t_L$$\end{document} giving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_c-x_L)/t_L=v_B$$\end{document}, as seen from Eq. (21).

Local work-energy theorem

The weak value of the Hamiltonian operator in Eq. (1) is not measurable, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{H}}$$\end{document} does not satisfy C1 as seen in Eq. (4). On the contrary, the kinetic energy operator defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{W}}:=\frac{1}{2}m\hat{{\textbf {V}}}^2$$\end{document} is gauge invariant. Then, we can use Eq. (15) to compute its RHD as:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left. \frac{\partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{W}},0,t_R\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )}{\partial t_R}\right| _{t_{1}=0}=q\;{\textbf {v}}_B^{|{\textbf {v}}\rangle }({\textbf {x}}) \cdot {\textbf {E}}({\textbf {x}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{W}}$$\end{document} is gauge invariant and satisfies C5 because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {v}}\rangle$$\end{document} is an eigenstate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{O}}=\hat{{W}}$$\end{document}. The details can be found in Online Appendices J and K. The expression (28) is just the local version (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {x}}$$\end{document}-density) of the corresponding global work-energy theorem. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q\;{\textbf {v}}_B^{|{\textbf {v}}\rangle }({\textbf {x}}) \cdot {\textbf {E}}({\textbf {x}})$$\end{document} is positive at some particular location \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {x}}$$\end{document}, one can interpret that the kinetic energy of the particle at such position increases, while decreases otherwise.

Numerical example

We show how an example of Eq. (28) can be implemented in a practical scenario. We consider a single electron with a uniform electric field in the x-direction, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {E}}= (E, 0, 0)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=-1\cdot 10^{6}$$\end{document} V/m. To proceed, we need to find a gauge potential for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document}. There is, of course, no unique choice. We select the usual Coulomb gauge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g=0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^g=-Ex$$\end{document}. Since the system becomes separable, to predict the electrical field from the time derivative of a weak value Eq. (28), we can focus only on the x component of such a weak value. Then, the 1D version of the Hamiltonian in Eq. (1) becomes just:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H=H({\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}},A)= -\frac{\hbar ^2}{2m^*}\frac{\partial ^2}{\partial x^2}+ q E x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}To evaluate RHD (in fact FDRHD), the initial (pre-selected) state has to be an eigenstate of the velocity operator that satisfies C5. We roughly approximate such eigenstate by the Gaussian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|\Phi _{5}(0) \rangle$$\end{document} described in Table 2. The wave packet spatial dispersion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\sigma }_{x}}=127$$\end{document} nm corresponds to a width in the reciprocal space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sigma }_{k} = 1/{\sigma }_{x}=7.8$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu m^{-1}$$\end{document}. Thus, the wave packet with a large spatial dispersion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sigma }_{x}\rightarrow \infty$$\end{document} ( \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sigma }_{k} \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}) mimics a plane wave, which is an effective eigenstate of the velocity operator.Fig. 3Time evolution of the modulus squared of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|\Phi _{5}(t) \rangle$$\end{document} (blue) in a time-independent potential energy profile (green). As mentioned, and as would also happen in a laboratory, in this numerical simulation the time derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial }{\partial t_R} Re \left \{ { \frac{\langle x | \frac{1}{2}m^*\hat{V}_x^2 \hat{U}_{{t_R}}|\Phi _{5} \rangle }{\langle x |\hat{U}_{{t_R}}|\Phi _{5} \rangle }}\right \}$$\end{document} is done through FDRHD in Eq. (19), instead of RHD, as they both give identical results because C5 is satisfied.Fig. 5In blue, mean value and standard deviation of the time derivative of Eq. Despite one position x and one time t being enough to predict E(x, t) through Eq. (31), we have enriched the figures by considering several times t and positions x where the weak values are computed. At each time, t, the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(x,t)|_{x=x_B^j(t)}$$\end{document} are numerically evaluated for a set of positions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=x_B^j(t)$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_B^j(t)$$\end{document} are the different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,...,10$$\end{document} Bohmian trajectories plotted in Fig. 3. The initial positions of the trajectories are selected according to the initial wave function probability distribution to ensure that they are relevant positions in this scenario at all times. The mean value and the standard deviation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(x,t)|_{x=x_B^j(t)}$$\end{document} for different times are indicated in Fig. 5. The same procedure is done in Fig. 4 to plot the mean value and the standard deviation of the weak values of the velocity and of the kinetic energy. The information on the standard deviation shows how robust the predictions are when different times and positions are considered in this example with a uniform and constant electric field.

We emphasize that the velocity at each position x and time t in the trajectories of Fig. 3 requires a specific weak measurement protocol that is independent of previous positions and times. In the laboratory, the computation of the weak value at each time t would assume that the initial (pre-selected) Gaussian wave packet is defined at such time t, i.e., the pre-selected state at t is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|\Phi _{5}(t) \rangle$$\end{document}. Therefore, the connection of different velocities in Fig. 4 to construct the continuous Bohmian trajectory shown in Fig. 3 is natural within the Bohmian interpretation. However, such a connection is not necessary in the ontologically-free approach used to develop the time-derivative weak values presented in this paper. In any case, it can be illustrative to provide an additional justification of the above results from a Bohmian perspective. The weak value of the kinetic energy in Eq. (30) can be rewritten in a Bohmian language as:32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Re \left \{ { \frac{\langle x | \frac{1}{2}m^*\hat{V}_x^2 |\Phi _{5} \rangle }{\langle x |\Phi _{5} \rangle }} \right \}=\frac{1}{2}m^* \left( v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)\right) ^2+Q^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t) \approx \frac{1}{2}m^* \left( v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)\right) ^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t))$$\end{document} is the so-called (Bohmian) quantum potential that depends on the curvature of the modulus of the wave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|\Phi _{5}(t) \rangle$$\end{document}. For the spatially large wave packet considered here, such a last contribution can be neglected in front of the first one. Then, the last term in Eq. (32) shows one possible procedure to evaluate the weak value of the kinetic energy in Eq. (30) for this particular scenario in the laboratory: once the velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)$$\end{document} is computed, the kinetic energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}m^* \left( v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)\right) ^2$$\end{document} in Eq. (32) can be obtained by numerical post-processing the empirically obtained Bohmian velocity by squaring the value and multiplying the result by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}m^*$$\end{document}9. Finally, the derivative in Eq. (31) can be written from Eq. (32) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d }{dt}\left( \frac{1}{2}m^* \left( v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)\right) ^2\right) \approx m^*\; v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)\; \frac{d v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)}{dt}$$\end{document}. Invoking again the negligible role of the quantum potential in this scenario (the spatial derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t))$$\end{document} is negligible in front of the spatial derivative of scalar potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^g=-Ex$$\end{document}), one recovers a Newton’s law for the Bohmian trajectory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^*\; \frac{d v_B^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(t)}{dt} \approx qE$$\end{document} to reach again Eq. (31) from a Bohmian perspective.

Finally, we present a summary of the protocol to be followed in a laboratory to obtain the electric field E(x, t) at a specific time t and a specific position x (corresponding to one of the orange points in Fig. 5). using FDRHD from Eq. (19) and the final numerical outputs obtained in steps 2) and 3).
