(31), using the output in 4) as an input and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)$$\end{document} empirically evaluated in step 1) as the other input.

Numerically evaluate the electric field E(x, t) from Eq. (31), using the output in 4) as an input and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B}^{|\Phi _{5}\rangle }(x,t)$$\end{document} empirically evaluated in step 1) as the other input.

Local Lorentz force

We consider a consecutive application of FDLHD in Eq. (18) and FDRHD in Eq. (19), ensuring the gauge invariance of all intermediate expressions, to get:33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m^*\left. \frac{\partial ^2 \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}},t_L,t_R\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )}{\partial t_R \partial t_L}\right| {\begin{matrix}_{ t_R=0}\\ _{t_L=0} \end{matrix}}=q({\textbf {E}}({\textbf {x}})+{\textbf {v}}_B^{|{\textbf {v}}\rangle } \times {\textbf {B}}({\textbf {x}})), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {E}}({\textbf {x}})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {B}}({\textbf {x}})$$\end{document} are the (gauge invariant) electric and magnetic fields at position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {x}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}}_B^{|{\textbf {v}}\rangle }$$\end{document} is the (gauge invariant) Bohmian velocity of the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {v}}\rangle$$\end{document} computed from Eq. (25). See Online Appendies L and M.

Notice that the second-order time derivative in Eq. (33) is, in fact, a compact way of referring to a first-order time derivative of another first-order time derivative. The output of the FDLHD given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. {\partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {X}}},t_L,t_R\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )}/{ \partial t_L}\right| _{t_L=0}=\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,t_R\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )$$\end{document} that satisfies C4 (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\hat{{O}},\hat{{F}}]=[\hat{{\textbf {X}}},\hat{{\textbf {X}}}]=0$$\end{document}), is used as the input of the FDRHD given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. {\partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\textbf {V}}},0,t_R\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )}/{\partial t_R}\right| _{ t_R=0}=\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{{\Xi }},0,0\big ||{\textbf {x}}\rangle ,|{\textbf {v}}\rangle \big )$$\end{document} that satisfies C5 (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\hat{{O}},\hat{\Lambda }]=[\hat{{\textbf {V}}},\hat{{\textbf {V}}}]=0$$\end{document}), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\Xi }}:=\frac{i}{\hbar }[\hat{{H}},\hat{{\textbf {V}}}]+\frac{\partial \hat{{\textbf {V}}}}{\partial t}$$\end{document} is defined in Online Appendix L. This sequence is precisely what occurs in the Ehrenfest theorem. Two consecutive first-order time derivatives, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}, can be compactly written together as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}. There are several possible finite-difference approximations to evaluate a mathematical second-order time derivative in Eq. (33). The technical differences among them is not relevant in our discussion7. The final result in Eq. (33) is simply a quantum and local version of the (Newton) Lorentz force, which can be used, for example, as a quantum sensor of electromagnetic fields at specific position and time as shown in the numerical example below.

Numerical example

We consider a quantum system with a magnetic field in the z-direction, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {B}}= (0, 0, B)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=0.19$$\end{document} Tesla, and no electric field. To proceed, we need to find a gauge potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g$$\end{document} which obeys \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {B}}=\nabla \times {\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g$$\end{document}. There is, of course, no unique choice. Here we pick \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}^g = (0, xB, 0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^g=0$$\end{document}. This is called the Landau gauge. In this scenario, the Lorentz force in Eq. (33) is:34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textbf {E}}({\textbf {x}})+{\textbf {v}}_B({\textbf {x}}) \times {\textbf {B}}({\textbf {x}})=\{\; v_{B,y} B, \;- v_{B,x} B,\; 0\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The dynamics of the z direction are not relevant in this scenario, so we assume that an electron moves only in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x-y$$\end{document} plane. Then, the Hamiltonian in Eq. (1) becomes:35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H=\frac{1}{2m^*}\left( -{\hbar ^2} \frac{\partial ^2}{\partial x^2} + \left( -i{\hbar } \frac{\partial }{\partial y} -qBx \right) ^2\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since this Hamiltonian Eq. (35) has translational invariance in the y direction, the component of the energy eigenstate in such direction will be a plane wave. On the other hand, it is well-known that the global energy eigenstates of Eq. (35) correspond to those of a displaced harmonic oscillator as described in Online Appendix N.Fig. 6Probability distribution in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x-y$$\end{document} space for the quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi _H(x,y,t)|^2$$\end{document} in Eq. (36) at times 0 and 0.2 ps. A set of Bohmian trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_B^j(t)$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,..,10$$\end{document} are computed by time-integrating the (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x-$$\end{document}velocity) weak value in Eq. (37) and the (y-velocity) weak value in Eq. (38).

Probability distribution in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x-y$$\end{document} space for the quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi _H(x,y,t)|^2$$\end{document} in Eq. (36) at times 0 and 0.2 ps. A set of Bohmian trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_B^j(t)$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,..,10$$\end{document} are computed by time-integrating the (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x-$$\end{document}velocity) weak value in Eq. Since we are interested in initial states that are eigenvalues of the velocity operator, we consider a superposition of 10 energy eigenstates, all with the same \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_y=0.0118$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${nm}^{-1}$$\end{document}:36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _H(x,y,t) = \sum _{n=0}^9 c_{n,k_y}(t) \psi _{n,k_y}(x,y), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{n,k_y}(x,y)$$\end{document} is defined in Eq. (N64) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{n,k_y}(t)$$\end{document} in Eq. (N65) in Online Appendix N. By construction, the state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _H(x,y,t)$$\end{document} in Eq. (36) is an eigenstate of the velocity in the y-direction (notice that the dependence on y of the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{n,k_y}(x,y)$$\end{document} is given by the global term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{ik_y y}$$\end{document} in Eq. (N64) in Online Appendix N), but not in the x direction. Thus, we focus on the y-component of Eq. (33) that gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-v_{B,x} B$$\end{document} in Eq. (34), allowing to predict B.Fig. 7In blue, mean value and standard deviation of the y-velocity of the 10 trajectories in Fig. 6 at 20 different times (left axis) computed from the weak value in Eq. In Fig. 6, we have plotted the probability distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi _H(x,y,t)|^2$$\end{document} at the preparation time (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=0$$\end{document} ps) and final (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=t_f=20$$\end{document} ps) times. The evaluation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _H(x,y,t)$$\end{document} at different times is explained in Online Appendix N. We also plot a set of 10 Bohmian trajectories \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_B(t)$$\end{document} whose velocity in the x-component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B,x}^{|\psi _H\rangle }(x,y,t)$$\end{document} is computed from the x-component of the weak value in Eq. (25) as:37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v_{B,x}^{|\psi _H\rangle }= Re \left \{ { \frac{\langle x,y| \hat{V}_x |\psi _H(t)\rangle }{\langle x,y |\psi _H(t)\rangle }} \right \}=\frac{\hbar }{m^*} Im \left( \frac{\frac{\partial \Psi _H(x,y,t)}{\partial x}}{\Psi _H(x,y,t)} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and velocity in the y-component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B,y}^{|\psi _H\rangle }(x,y,t)$$\end{document} is computed from the y-component of the weak value in Eq. (27). As discussed for the local work-energy theorem, only one position in configuration space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x,y\}$$\end{document} and one time t would have been enough to compute the magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=B(x,y,t)$$\end{document}. We consider several times and several positions (i.e., the Bohmian trajectories depicted in Fig. 6) to enrich the discussion and to test how robust these predictions are in front of variations of positions and times. (38) are plotted in blue in Figs. 7 and 8. Finally, in Fig. 8, we have plotted the predicted value of the magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=B(x,y,t)$$\end{document} through the y-component of the Eq. (M48), in Online Appendix M, in the particular case of Eq. (34), giving:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B(x,y,t)\approx & -\frac{m^*}{q}\frac{\left. \frac{ \partial ^2 \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{y},t_L,t_R\big ||x,y\rangle ,|\psi _H\rangle \big )}{\partial t_L \partial t_R}\right| {\begin{matrix}_{ t_R=0}\\ _{t_L=0} \end{matrix}}}{\left. \frac{ \partial \mathcal {W}\big (\hat{x},t_L,0\big ||x,y\rangle ,|\psi _H\rangle \big )}{\partial t_L}\right| _{t_L=0}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}There is an excellent agreement between the magnetic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=0.19$$\end{document} Tesla and the value predicted through the time derivative of weak values in Eq. (31). Notice that the last identity in Eq. (39) shows that, in the laboratory, only the weak value of the x-position, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{x},t_L,0\big ||x,y\rangle ,|\psi _H\rangle$$\end{document}, and y-position, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}\big (\hat{y},t_L,t_R\big ||x,y\rangle ,|\psi _H\rangle$$\end{document} needs to be evaluated at different times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_R$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_L$$\end{document}. The result in Eq. (39) can be alternatively justified from a Bohmian perspective, as follows. The first term of the velocity in the y direction in Eq. (38) can be considered roughly as a constant for a plane wave, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\hbar }{m^*} Im \left( {\frac{\partial \Psi _H(x,y,t)}{\partial y}}/{\Psi _H(x,y,t)}\right) =\frac{\hbar k_y}{m^*}$$\end{document}, where the mentioned quantum potential becomes negligible. Thus, such velocity can be written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{B,y}^{|\psi _H\rangle }(t)={\hbar k_y}/{m^*}-q B x_B(t)/m^*$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_B(t)$$\end{document} is the x-component of the Bohmian trajectory. Finally, the acceleration (derivative) of this Bohmian velocity in Eq. (38) becomes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d v_{B,y}^{|\psi _H\rangle }(x,y,t)}{d t}=-\frac{q\; B\; }{m^*}\frac{d x_{B}(t)}{dt} =-\frac{q\; B\; }{m^*}v_{B,x}^{|\psi _H\rangle }(t)$$\end{document} which is another way of rewriting the expected result in Eq. (39).

Which modal theories exhibit time-dependent consistency of weak values?

In the results presented in the previous Section “Examples on the time derivative of weak values”, we have shown that the weak values post-selected in position and its time derivative can be linked to the physical properties of the Bohmian theory. For example, the weak value of the velocity post-selected in position gives the Bohmian velocity of a particle5. Thus, it seems that the Bohmian theory is a good framework for predicting weak values post-selected in position and its time derivative. However, Ref.77 explicitly shows that other Bohm-like theories can be used to predict weak values post-selected in eigenstates other than position eigenstates. The reason why there is an infinite number of Bohm-like theories (also known as modal theories23) that can provide a consistent picture of quantum phenomena in terms of weak values can be easily evidenced by unraveling the expectation value of the position operator in terms of weak values with an arbitrary post-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{f}\rangle$$\end{document}. From the identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle = \langle \psi | \int d{f}|{f}\rangle \langle {f}| \hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}, we get:40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle= & \int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Thus, interpreting the expectation value as a weighted sum of weak values post-selected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}$$\end{document} is consistent with Eq. (40). The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Re \{..\}$$\end{document} ensures that the expectation value has no imaginary part. Therefore, each set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{|{f}\rangle \}$$\end{document} that satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int d{f}|{f}\rangle \langle {f}|=1\!\!1$$\end{document} can be identified as the primary ontological element of a new Bohm-like theory (i.e., a new \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}$$\end{document}-modal theory). In particular, the authors of Ref.77 define the traditional Bohmian theory, where position is the primary ontology, as the x-Bohm theory. Alternatively, they define a p-Bohm theory as a Bohm-like theory where velocity (momentum) is the primary ontological element (i.e., the p-Bohm theory can be used to predict weak values post-selected in velocity eigenstates satisfying Eq. (40)). By comparing the phase-space dynamics offered by the x-Bohm and p-Bohm theories for an arbitrary pre-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi$$\end{document}, the authors of Ref.77 conclude that our previous argument that the x-Bohm theory provides a consistent explanation of quantum phenomena is quite speculative. Their argument does not negate that the x-Bohm theory provides a consistent explanation (i.e., weak values post-selected in position satisfy Eq. (40)), but rather that the x-Bohm theory is merely one consistent explanation among an infinite number of possible consistent, yet distinct, Bohm-like explanations of quantum phenomena (i.e., weak values post-selected in any eigenstate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{f}\rangle$$\end{document} that also satisfy Eq. (40)). The summary of the argumentation of Ref.77 is the following: “When everyone is somebody, then no one’s anybody”.

The developments elaborated in this paper on time derivatives of weak values using LHD and RHD also establish an additional and more restrictive criterion on which post-selected states exhibit also a time-dependent consistency. The Ehrenfest theorem deduced from Eq. (9) shows the following time-dependent relationship \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document} for any quantum state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}. Therefore, it seems reasonable to demand a similar time-dependent consistency for the weak value that unravels the time derivative of the expectation values. For the position operator, this additional criterion can be written as:41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle =\int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2 Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\nonumber \\ & {\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\text{?? }}}}\int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2\left. \frac{\partial }{\partial t_L} Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\right| _{t_L=0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}We have shown in Section “Time Derivative of Weak values” that the accomplishment of the last identity in Eq. (41), which we named time-dependent consistent criterion, is not true, in general, for arbitrary post-selected states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|$$\end{document}. The identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. \frac{\partial }{\partial t_L} Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {X}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\right| _{t_L=0}=Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }}\right \}$$\end{document} holds only for very specific post-selected states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|$$\end{document}. In the particular case of the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document}, from C4, we have shown that only the weak values post-selected in position, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{f}\rangle =|x\rangle$$\end{document}, satisfy both Eq. (40) and Eq. (41) for an arbitrary pre-selected state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}. See Online Appendix O for an alternative demonstration of this result. Thus, the Bohmian theory has a quite privileged status when dealing with weak value post-selected in position and their time derivatives. A similar time-dependent consistent criterion can be obtained by replacing the left-hand derivative (LHD) with the right-hand derivative (RHD) in Eq. (41), and ensuring condition C5, at the price of fixing the pre-selected state as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle = |{\textbf {x}}\rangle$$\end{document}.

This previous criterion, on which pre-selected states have time-dependent consistency, is an operator-dependent criterion. By replacing the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {X}}}$$\end{document} in Eq. (41) by another operator, for example \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}, other post-selected states are satisfying the time-dependent consistent criterion. Again, we notice that there is an infinite set of arbitrarily post-selected states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{f}\rangle$$\end{document} satisfying:42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle= & \int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The requirement imposed by the Ehrenfest theorem deduced from Eq. (9) is now \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\Xi }}:= \frac{i}{\hbar }[\hat{{H}},\hat{{\textbf {V}}}] + \frac{\partial \hat{{\textbf {V}}}}{\partial t}$$\end{document} being the operator defined in Online Appendix L. The Ehrenfest theorem can be translated as an additional requirement for the time-dependent consistency of the weak values as follows:43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{d}{dt}\langle \psi |\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle =\langle \psi |\hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle =\int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2 Re\left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\nonumber \\ & {\mathop {=}\limits ^{{\text{?? }}}}\int d{f}|\langle \psi |{f}\rangle |^2\left. \frac{d}{d t_L} Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\right| _{t_L=0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}, from C4, we have shown that weak values post-selected in velocity (momentum), i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {f}|=\langle {\textbf {v}}|$$\end{document}, satisfy both Eq. (42) and Eq. (43) as they accomplish \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left. \frac{d}{d t_L} Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{U}_{{t_L}}\hat{{\textbf {V}}}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\hat{U}_{{t_L}}|\psi \rangle }} \right \}\right| _{t_L=0}=Re \left \{ {\frac{\langle {f}| \hat{{\Xi }}|\psi \rangle }{\langle {f}|\psi \rangle }}\right \}$$\end{document} for arbitrary post-selected states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle$$\end{document}. A similar time-dependent consistent criterion can be obtained by changing LHD by RHD in Eq. (43) and ensuring condition C5, at the price of fixing the pre-selected state as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\psi \rangle =|{\textbf {v}}\rangle$$\end{document}.

In principle, it appears that only the first-order time derivative of weak values can be obtained because the post-selected states in Eq. (41) differ from those in Eq. (43). However, by using the equality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Re \left \{ {{\langle {\textbf {v}}| \hat{{\textbf {V}}}| x\rangle }/{\langle {\textbf {v}}|x \rangle }} \right \}=\left \{ Re {{\langle x | \hat{{\textbf {V}}}| {\textbf {v}}\rangle }/{\langle x |{\textbf {v}}\rangle }} \right \}$$\end{document}, which follows from the hermiticity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{{\textbf {V}}}$$\end{document}, both Eqs. (41) and (43) can still be satisfied by considering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle x|$$\end{document} as the post-selected state and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {v}}\rangle$$\end{document} as the pre-selected state. This is what happens in the numerical results in Section “Local Lorentz force”.

Conclusions

In physics, the time derivative of a property frequently gives rise to another meaningful property. For example, in classical mechanics, the knowledge of the position allows one to predict the value of the velocity without measuring it, just by taking a first-order time derivative of the position. Even the acceleration of a system (related to the forces acting on the system) can be anticipated from the second-order time derivatives of the position. Since weak values offer empirical insights that cannot be derived from expectation values, this paper investigates what physical insight can be obtained from the time derivative of weak values.

A time derivative of a weak value is not always either a weak value or a gauge-invariant quantity. The necessary and sufficient conditions for the gauge invariance (i.e., empirical observability) of weak values are presented in this paper, i.e., conditions C1, C2 and C3. There are weak values in the literature that do not satisfy these conditions, as shown in Table 1 and discussed in Section “Time Derivative of Weak values”. In addition, two more necessary and sufficient conditions (C4, C5) are presented to ensure that the time derivative of a gauge-invariant weak value also becomes a gauge-invariant weak value. In particular, condition C4 applies to LHD and condition C5 to RHD.

We have also discussed how the LHD and RHD can be empirically evaluated in the laboratory through finite-difference approximations of the left- and right-hand time derivatives of weak values, referred to as FDLHD and FDRHD, respectively. We show that FDLHD and FDRHD yield results that are roughly identical to the theoretical LHD and RHD when the latter are gauge invariant.

The time-derivative of weak values can be used, for example, to deduce a local Ehrenfest-like theorem as follows: one weak value (position) measured in the laboratory provides information on two other unmeasured weak values obtained through first-order (velocity) and second-order (acceleration) time derivatives of the first measured weak value. As an example, an electromagnetic field quantum sensor at a local position and time is proposed, utilizing the empirical evaluation of weak values of the position, and computing their time derivatives (through FDLHD and FDRHD).

The findings in this paper reveal an important asymmetry between different post-selected states used to compute weak values. If we want to describe the local position, local velocity, and local acceleration of quantum systems in terms of weak values, and we want these weak values also to satisfy the additional time-dependent consistency criteria explained in Eqs. (41) and (43), we conclude that weak values post-selected in position, and somehow also post-selected in velocity, play a quite privileged role—one that cannot be attributed to other weak values post-selected in other quantum states. This privilege, however, implies a limitation on the types of scenarios that are empirically accessible for obtaining simultaneous insight into the local dynamics of quantum systems through second-order time derivatives of weak values of the position. This second-order time derivative can be used in scenarios pre-selected in velocity eigenstates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {v}}\rangle$$\end{document} and post-selected in position eigenstates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|$$\end{document} (or vice versa). In any case, the second-order time derivative of the weak values involving these wave functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle {\textbf {x}}|{\textbf {v}}(t)\rangle$$\end{document} is enough to “detect” forces, in general, and electromagnetic fields, in particular.

In this paper, the electric and magnetic fields are treated as external elements of the single-particle Schrödinger equation: the simulated particle used to compute the weak value (which later senses the electromagnetic fields) is affected by these fields, but the fields themselves are not affected by the particle. This approach is the so-called semi-classical treatment of light and matter. The possibility of measuring how electromagnetic fields are distributed in space and time demonstrates the richness of using local-in-position weak values, rather than ensemble values, to characterize physical systems. Future work aims to extend the ideas discussed here to weak values in which both the simulated particle (i.e., matter) and the electromagnetic fields (i.e., light) are quantized8.

We emphasize that no fundamental (ontological) conclusions can be drawn from the results of this paper, as all mathematical predictions presented here hold under any interpretation of quantum mechanics. The purpose of this work is not to address the ontological meaning of weak values but rather to investigate their time derivatives and practical applicability. Similarly, the privileged connection between weak values post-selected in position and the Bohmian properties of a quantum system is not intended to argue that Bohmian mechanics is true, but only to highlight its usefulness in envisioning empirical protocols for characterizing novel local properties of quantum systems.
